\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
-rank 1.

Lemma 1.12
.

Suppose
T
is pregeometric.
Then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
is the coarsest real-strict independence relation on
\mathbb{M}
, and so coincides with real þ-forking independence.

Proof.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}
be a real-strict independence relation on
\mathbb{M}
.
By monotonicity and finite character, it suffices to show that
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B\implies A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
for all finite
A
.
Let us first suppose that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
for a single element
a\in\mathbb{M}
.
If
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(a|BC)=1
, then
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(a|C)=1
as well.
On the other hand, if
a\in\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(BC)
, then strong closure gives
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(aC)\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(C)}\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(BC)
, monotonicity gives
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(C)}a
, and real-strictness gives
a\in\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(C)
.
In both cases, we have
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
.

Now we show that
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B\implies A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
for
A
finite by induction on the size of
A
.
Suppose this holds for a given
A
, and let
a\in\mathbb{M}
with
Aa\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
.
Normality, base monotonicity, and monotonicity give
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{AC}B
(where we use symmetry to apply base monotonicity on the left).
Our induction hypothesis gives
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
, and the singleton case gives
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{AC}B
.
Applying normality, we have
AC\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
and
aAC\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{AC}B
, and applying transitivity yields
aAC\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
.
We conclude by monotonicity.
∎

Gagelman defines a
surgical theory
to be a pregeometric theory with the additional property that for any definable equivalence relation
E
on a definable set
X
, only finitely many
E
-classes have the same
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}
-dimension as
X

[
23
]
.
It is folklore that a theory
T
is surgical if and only if it is superrosy of þ-rank 1; we provide a proof in Proposition
A.4
below.
Accordingly, we use
surgical
to mean
superrosy of þ-rank 1
.
If
T
is surgical, then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B\iff A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
for all
A,B,C\subseteq\mathbb{M}
.
If
T
is pregeometric with GEI, then
T
is surgical by
[
23
, Corollary 3.6]
.
Being pregeometric alone is not enough:

Example 1.13
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be an algebraically closed valued field with a
nontrivial valuation.
Then
T
is geometric and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
is real-strict and real-canonical, but
T
is not rosy.

For simple theories with elimination of hyperimaginaries (for example, stable and supersimple theories), forking independence coincides with þ-forking independence; see
[
1
, Corollary 3.4]
or
[
38
, Theorem 5.1.4]
.
By Proposition
A.4
, this gives:

Fact 1.14
.

If
T
is supersimple with
SU(T)=1
, then
T
is surgical and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
.

The SU-rank 1 assumption is necessary:

Example 1.15
.

Let
\mathbb{M}=(M;E)
, where
M
is a set and
E
is an equivalence relation on
M
with infinitely many equivalence classes, all infinite.
Then
T
is
\omega
-stable of Morley rank 2, geometric, and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
is real-strict and real-canonical, but
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\neq\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
.

2.
Geometric theories of fields

We assume for the rest of this note that
T
is a pregeometric theory extending the
\mathcal{L}_{\ring}
-theory of fields.
Throughout this section,
independence
refers to
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}
-independence (equivalently,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
-independence).
Given a definable set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{M}^{n}
, defined over a small set of parameters
A
, we set

\dim_{\mathcal{L}}(Y)\coloneqq\max\{\rk_{\mathcal{L}}({\bar{a}}|A):{\bar{a}}\in Y\}.

Then
\dim_{\mathcal{L}}(Y)
is independent of the specific choice of defining parameters.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be an infinite definable set.
For
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}
, we let

\Inv_{X}(\alpha)\coloneqq\{(a,b,c,d)\in X^{4}:(a-d)/(b-c)=\alpha\}.

Given
S\subseteq\mathbb{M}
, we let
\Inv_{X}(S)\coloneqq\bigcup_{\alpha\in S}\Inv_{X}(\alpha)
.
In
[
21
, Lemma 3.47]
, it is shown that
\Inv_{X}(\alpha)
is nonempty for each
\alpha
; see also
[
11
,
26
]
.
It follows that
T
eliminates
\exists^{\infty}
, and so we refer to
T
as a
geometric theory of fields
.
Since
T
is geometric,
\dim_{\mathcal{L}}
is a definable dimension function in the sense of
[
45
]
.
Note that
\dim_{\mathcal{L}}\Inv_{X}(\alpha)\leqslant 3
; this is even an equality:

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}
and let
(a,b)\in X^{2}
be independent over
\alpha
and the defining parameters for
X
.
Then the fiber

\Inv_{X}(\alpha)_{a,b}=\{(c,d)\in X^{2}:(a-d)/(b-c)=\alpha\}

is infinite.
In particular,
\Inv_{X}(\alpha)
has dimension 3.

Proof.

Fix a set of parameters
A
such that
X
is
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable and
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(a,b|A,\alpha)=2
.
We have
(c,d)\in\Inv_{X}(\alpha)_{a,b}
if and only if
a-b\alpha=d-c\alpha
and
(c,d)\neq(b,a)
.
Thus, it suffices to show that the set
\{(c,d)\in X^{2}:a-b\alpha=d-c\alpha\}
is infinite.
This holds since
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(a,b|A,\alpha,a-b\alpha)=1
.
∎

Now let
\mathcal{Y}=(Y_{z})_{z\in Z}
be a definable family of infinite subsets of
\mathbb{M}
.
We allow the indexing set
Z
to be a definable set of imaginaries.
For
a,b\in X
and
z\in Z
, we let

X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z}\coloneqq\{c\in X:X\cap(a+(c-b)Y_{z})\text{ is infinite}\}.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
(a,b)\in X^{2}
be independent over the defining parameters for
X
and
\mathcal{Y}
.
Then for any
z\in Z
, the set
X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z}
is infinite.

Proof.

Fix
z\in Z
and a set of parameters
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
such that
X
and
\mathcal{Y}
are
\mathcal{L}(A)
-definable and
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(a,b|A)=2
.
Let
\alpha\in Y_{z}
with
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(\alpha|A,a,b)=1
, so
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(a,b|A,\alpha)=2
.
Thus
\Inv_{X}(\alpha)_{a,b}
is infinite by Lemma
2.1
, so take
(c,d)\in\Inv_{X}(\alpha)_{a,b}
with
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(c,d|A,\alpha,a,b)=1
.
Then
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(c,d|A,a,b)=\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(c,d,\alpha|A,a,b)=2
, so

\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(c|A,a,b)=\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(d|A,a,b,c)=1.

The intersection
X\cap(a+(c-b)Y_{z})
is infinite, since it contains
d=a+(c-b)\alpha
.
Thus,
c\in X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z}
, so this set is infinite as well.
∎

2.1.
Stable geometric fields are strongly minimal

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be infinite and definable over
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
.
Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}^{\times}
and suppose that for all
\beta,\gamma\in X
with
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(\beta,\gamma|A,\alpha)=2
, we have
\beta-\gamma,\alpha\beta/\gamma\in X
.
Then
X
is cofinite in
\mathbb{M}
.

Proof.

Fix some
\delta\in\mathbb{M}
with
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(\delta|A,\alpha)=1
.
It suffices to show that
\delta\in X
.
Using Lemma
2.1
, take
(a,b,c,d)\in\Inv_{X}(\alpha^{-1}\delta)
with
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(a,b,c,d|A,\alpha,\delta)\geqslant 3
.
Then
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(a,b,c,d|A,\alpha)=\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(a,b,c,d,\delta|A,\alpha)=4
.
Applying our hypothesis gives
a-d,b-c\in X
, and so
\delta=\alpha(a-d)/(b-c)\in X
as well.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be an infinite, coinfinite definable set and let
\Aff(X)=(a+bX)_{a\in\mathbb{M},b\in\mathbb{M}^{\times}}
be the definable family of affine translates of
X
.
Let
Z
be an infinite set given by a finite intersection of sets in
\Aff(X)
and complements of sets in
\Aff(X)
.
Then there is
Y\in\Aff(X)
such that
Z\cap Y
and
Z\setminus Y
are both infinite.

Proof.

Suppose towards contradiction that we have such a set
Z
for which there is no
Y\in\Aff(X)
with both
Z\cap Y
and
Z\setminus Y
infinite.
Take a set
A
of parameters over which
Z
is defined.
Note that if
W
is a finite intersection of sets in
\Aff(X)
and complements of sets in
\Aff(X)
, then either
Z\cap W
or
Z\setminus W
is finite.
Let
\delta\in Z
with
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(\delta|A)=1
.
We will show that
Z^{\prime}\coloneqq Z-\delta
is cofinite in
\mathbb{M}
.
From this, it follows that
X
is either finite or cofinite, a contradiction.
Note that
Z^{\prime}
satisfies the same hypothesis as
Z
.

We verify the conditions in Lemma
2.3
.
Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in Z^{\prime}
with
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma|A,\delta)=3
.
We need to show that
\beta-\gamma,\alpha\beta/\gamma\in Z^{\prime}
.
First, note that
\gamma+\delta\in Z\cap(\gamma+Z)
.
Since
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(\gamma+\delta|A,\gamma)=1
, we see that
Z\cap(\gamma+Z)
is infinite, and thus cofinite in
Z
, since
\gamma+Z
is a finite intersection of sets and complements of sets in
\Aff(X)
.
It follows that
Z^{\prime}\cap(\gamma+Z^{\prime})
is cofinite in
Z^{\prime}
, so it contains
\beta
.
In particular,
\beta-\gamma\in Z^{\prime}
.

Next, note that
\gamma\in Z^{\prime}\cap(\gamma/\alpha)Z^{\prime}
.
As before, we have
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(\gamma|A,\delta,\gamma/\alpha)=1
, so
Z^{\prime}\cap(\gamma/\alpha)Z^{\prime}
is infinite and thus cofinite in
Z^{\prime}
.
Thus,
\beta\in(\gamma/\alpha)Z^{\prime}
, yielding
\alpha\beta/\gamma\in Z^{\prime}
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 2.5
.

If
T
is stable, then it is strongly minimal.

Proof.

Suppose that
T
is not strongly minimal and let
X\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be an infinite and coinfinite definable set.
For each finite binary sequence
\nu\in 2^{<\omega}
, we choose an element
Y_{\nu}\in\Aff(X)
as follows: let
Y_{\emptyset}\coloneqq X
, assume we’ve defined
Y_{\eta}
for
\eta\in 2^{\leqslant n}
and that for all
\nu\in 2^{n+1}
, the set

Z_{\nu}\coloneqq\bigcap_{i\leqslant n,\nu(i)=0}(\mathbb{M}\setminus Y_{\nu|_{i}})\cap\bigcap_{i\leqslant n,\nu(i)=1}Y_{\nu|_{i}}

is infinite (this holds for
n=0
as
X
and
\mathbb{M}\setminus X
are both infinite).
Given
\nu\in 2^{n+1}
, we use Lemma
2.4
, to find
Y_{\nu}\in\Aff(X)
such that
Z_{\nu}\cap Y_{\nu}
and
Z_{\nu}\setminus Y_{\nu}
are both infinite.
Doing this for all
\nu\in 2^{<\omega}
, we see that
\Aff(X)
has the binary tree property.
Thus
T
is unstable; see
[
44
, Definition 8.2.1 and Theorem 8.2.3]
.
∎

Combining this result with
[
24
, Theorem 1]
, we immediately obtain:

Corollary 2.6
.

Any algebraically bounded stable field is an expansion of an algebraically closed field by constants (see Section
3
for a precise definition of algebraic boundedness).

We remark that we cannot relax the conditions here: by
[
24
, Theorem 2 and Lemma 3]
, there is a strongly minimal (thus, geometric) theory
T
with two field structures of different characteristics (other similar fusion constructions exist as well).

2.2.
Simple geometric fields are supersimple of SU-rank 1

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a definable family and let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{>1}
.
We define
D^{*}(\mathcal{Y},k)
as follows: for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
D^{*}(\mathcal{Y},k)\geqslant n
if we can find sets
(Y_{\eta})_{\emptyset\neq\eta\in\omega^{\leqslant n}}
from
\mathcal{Y}
such that

(1)

For all
\eta\in\omega^{<n}
, the family
(Y_{\eta,i})_{i<\omega}
is
k
-inconsistent;

(2)

For all
\mu\in\omega^{n}
, the set
\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}Y_{\eta}
is infinite.

In the intersection above, we use
\eta\trianglelefteq\mu
to indicate that
\eta
is an initial segment of
\mu
.
In the case
n=0
, both conditions hold trivially, so
D^{*}(\mathcal{Y},k)\geqslant 0
.
If
D^{*}(\mathcal{Y},k)\geqslant n
for all
n
, then
T
is not simple.

Theorem 2.7
.

Suppose
T
is simple.
Then
T
is supersimple of SU-rank 1.

Proof.

We prove the contrapositive: let
\mathcal{Y}=(Y_{z})_{z\in Z}
be a definable family of subsets of
\mathbb{M}
with each
Y_{z}
infinite and suppose that some member of this family
k
-divides.
We will show that
T
is not simple.
We consider two cases:

Case 1:

Suppose that for every infinite definable
X\subseteq\mathbb{M}
, every infinite sequence
(z_{i})_{i<\omega}
from
Z
, and every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
a,b,c\in X
such that the set
\{i<\omega:c\in X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}}\}
has cardinality at least
\ell
.
Consider the definable family

\Aff(\mathcal{Y})\coloneqq(a+bY_{z})_{(a,b,z)\in\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{\times}\times Z}

We will show that
D^{*}(\Aff(\mathcal{Y}),k)\geqslant n
for all natural numbers
n
by induction.
Suppose we’ve already shown this for a given
n
, so we have sets
(\tilde{Y}_{\eta})_{\emptyset\neq\eta\in\omega^{\leqslant n}}
from
\Aff(\mathcal{Y})
such that

(1)

for
\eta\in\omega^{<n}
, the family
(\tilde{Y}_{\eta,i})_{i<\omega}
is
k
-inconsistent, and

(2)

for all
\mu\in\omega^{n}
, the set
\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}\tilde{Y}_{\eta}
is infinite.

Let
\mu\in\omega^{n}
be given and set
X\coloneqq\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}\tilde{Y}_{\eta}
.
Using our case assumption, the fact that some
Y_{z}

k
-divides, and saturation, we find a sequence
(z_{i})_{i<\omega}
from
Z
and elements
a,b,c\in X
such that
c\in X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}}
for each
i
and
(Y_{z_{i}})_{i<\omega}
is
k
-inconsistent.
We set
\tilde{Y}_{\mu,i}\coloneqq a+(c-b)Y_{z_{i}}
for each
i
.
Then the family
(\tilde{Y}_{\mu,i})_{i<\omega}
is
k
-inconsistent as well, and for each
i
, the intersection
X\cap\tilde{Y}_{\mu,i}=X\cap(a+(c-b)Y_{z_{i}})
is infinite.
Defining
\tilde{Y}_{\mu,i}
in this way for all
\mu\in\omega^{n}
and all
i<\omega
, we see that
D^{*}(\Aff(\mathcal{Y}),k)\geqslant n+1
, as desired.

Case 2:

Suppose that there is an infinite definable set
X
, an infinite sequence
(z_{i})_{i<\omega}
from
Z
, and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
(a,b,c)\in X^{3}
, the set
\{i<\omega:c\in X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}}\}
has cardinality
<\ell
.
Then for all
(a,b)\in X^{2}
, the family
(X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}})_{i<\omega}
is
\ell
-inconsistent.
Consider the definable family
\mathcal{X}\coloneqq(X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z})_{(a,b,z)\in X^{2}\times Z}
.
We will show that
D^{*}(\mathcal{X},\ell)\geqslant n
for all natural numbers
n
by induction.
Suppose we’ve already shown this for a given
n
, so we have sets
(\tilde{X}_{\eta})_{\emptyset\neq\eta\in\omega^{\leqslant n}}
from
\mathcal{X}
such that

(1)

for
\eta\in\omega^{<n}
, the family
(\tilde{X}_{\eta,i})_{i<\omega}
is
\ell
-inconsistent, and

(2)

for all
\mu\in\omega^{n}
, the set
\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}\tilde{X}_{\eta}
is infinite.

Let
\mu\in\omega^{n}
be given and let
W\coloneqq\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}\tilde{X}_{\eta}
.
Let
(a,b)\in W^{2}
be independent over the defining parameters for
W
and
\mathcal{Y}
.
For each
i
, we set
\tilde{X}_{\mu,i}\coloneqq X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}}
.
Then
(\tilde{X}_{\mu,i})_{i<\omega}
is
\ell
-inconsistent, and for each
i
, we have

W\cap\tilde{X}_{\mu,i}=W\cap X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}}\supseteq W^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}},

and the set
W^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}}
is infinite by Lemma
2.2
.
This shows that
D^{*}(\mathcal{X},\ell)\geqslant n+1
, as desired.
∎

Applying Fact
1.14
, we have:

Corollary 2.8
.

Suppose that
T
is simple.
Then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{f}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
, so
SU({\bar{a}}|A)=\rk_{\mathcal{L}}({\bar{a}}|A)
for
{\bar{a}}\in\mathbb{M}^{n}
.

2.3.
Rosy geometric fields are surgical

The rosy case is very similar to the simple case, though some extra bookkeeping involving index sets is needed.
Let
\mathcal{Y}=(Y_{z})_{z\in Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}=(Z_{v})_{v\in V}
be definable families in
\mathbb{M}^{\eq}
where each
Z_{v}
is a subset of
Z
.
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{>1}
.
We define
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},k)
as follows: for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},k)\geqslant n
if we can find sets
(Z_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<n}}
from
\mathcal{Z}
and
(Y_{\eta})_{\emptyset\neq\eta\in\omega^{\leqslant n}}
from
\mathcal{Y}
such that

(1)

For all
\eta\in\omega^{<n}
, the sets
(Y_{\eta,i})_{i<\omega}
are pairwise distinct members of the family
(Y_{z})_{z\in Z_{\eta}}
, and this family is
k
-inconsistent;

(2)

For all
\mu\in\omega^{n}
, the set
\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}Y_{\eta}
is infinite.

In the case
n=0
, both conditions hold trivially, so
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},k)\geqslant 0
.

Fact 2.9
.

If there are
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{Z}
,
k
for which
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},k)\geqslant n
for all
n
, then
T
is not rosy.

Proof.

Let
\varphi(x,y)
be the formula defining the family
\mathcal{Y}
and let
\theta(y,z)
be the formula defining
\mathcal{Z}
.
Then
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},k)\leqslant\textnormal{\T1\thh}(x=x,\varphi,\theta,k)
, where the latter is Onshuus’ þ-rank
[
38
, Definition 3.1]
; see also the proof of
[
38
, Remark 3.1.2]
.
Thus, if
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},k)\geqslant n
for all
n
, then the same holds for
\textnormal{\T1\thh}(x=x,\varphi,\theta,k)
, so
T
is not rosy.
∎

Theorem 2.10
.

Suppose
T
is rosy.
Then
T
is surgical.

Proof.

We prove the contrapositive: let
\mathcal{Y}=(Y_{z})_{z\in Z}
be a definable family of subsets of
\mathbb{M}
with each
Y_{z}
infinite and suppose that some member of this family þ-divides.
We will show that
T
is not rosy.
After shrinking
Z
, we may assume that
\mathcal{Y}
is
k
-inconsistent for some
k
.
For
(a,b)\in\mathbb{M}^{2}
, we let
\tilde{Z}_{a,b}=\{(a,b)\}\times Z
.
We consider two cases:

Case 1:
Suppose that for all infinite definable
X\subseteq\mathbb{M}
, there are
(a,b,c)\in X^{3}
such that the set
\{z\in Z:c\in X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z}\}
is infinite.
Consider the definable families

\Aff(\mathcal{Y})\coloneqq(a+bY_{z})_{(a,b,z)\in\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{\times}\times Z},\qquad\mathcal{Z}^{\times}\coloneqq(\tilde{Z}_{a,b})_{(a,b)\in\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}^{\times}}.

We will show that
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\Aff(\mathcal{Y}),\mathcal{Z}^{\times},k)\geqslant n
for all natural numbers
n
by induction.
Suppose we’ve already shown this for a given
n
, so we have sets
(\tilde{Z}_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<n}}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{\times}
as well as sets
(\tilde{Y}_{\eta})_{\emptyset\neq\eta\in\omega^{\leqslant n}}
from
\Aff(\mathcal{Y})
where

(1)

for
\eta\in\omega^{<n}
, the sets
(\tilde{Y}_{\eta,i})_{i<\omega}
are pairwise distinct members of the
k
-inconsistent family
(\tilde{Y}_{w})_{w\in\tilde{Z}_{\eta}}
, and

(2)

for
\mu\in\omega^{n}
, the set
\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}\tilde{Y}_{\eta}
is infinite.

Let
\mu\in\omega^{n}
be given, and set
X\coloneqq\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}\tilde{Y}_{\eta}
.
Using our case assumption, let
(a,b,c)\in X^{3}
be such that the set
\{z\in Z:c\in X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z}\}
is infinite, and let
(z_{i})_{i<\omega}
be distinct elements of this set.
We set

\tilde{Z}_{\mu}\coloneqq\tilde{Z}_{a,c-b},\qquad\tilde{Y}_{\mu,i}=a+(c-b)Y_{z_{i}}\text{ for each $i$}.

Then the family
(\tilde{Y}_{w})_{w\in\tilde{Z}_{\mu}}=(a+(c-b)Y_{z})_{z\in Z}
is
k
-inconsistent and the intersection

X\cap\tilde{Y}_{\mu,i}=X\cap(a+(c-b)Y_{z_{i}})

is infinite for each
i
.
Defining
(\tilde{Z}_{\mu})_{\mu\in\omega^{n}}
and
(\tilde{Y}_{\mu,i})_{(\mu,i)\in\omega^{n}\times\omega}
in this way for all
\mu\in\omega^{n}
, we have shown that
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\Aff(\mathcal{Y}),\mathcal{Z}^{\times},k)\geqslant n+1
, as desired.

Case 2:

Now, suppose that we have an infinite definable
X\subseteq\mathbb{M}
such that for all
(a,b,c)\in X^{3}
, the set
\{z\in Z:c\in X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z}\}
is finite.
As we are working in a saturated model, we obtain a natural number
\ell
such that
\{z\in Z:c\in X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z}\}
has fewer than
\ell
elements for all
(a,b,c)\in X^{3}
.
Thus, for all
(a,b)\in X^{2}
, the family
(X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z})_{z\in Z}
is
\ell
-inconsistent.
Consider the definable families

\mathcal{X}\coloneqq(X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z})_{(a,b,z)\in X^{2}\times Z},\qquad\mathcal{Z}_{X}\coloneqq(\tilde{Z}_{a,b})_{(a,b)\in X^{2}}.

This time, we will show that
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Z}_{X},\ell)\geqslant n
for all natural numbers
n
by induction.
Suppose we’ve already shown this for a given
n
, so we have sets
(\tilde{Z}_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<n}}
from
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
as well as sets
(\tilde{X}_{\eta})_{\emptyset\neq\eta\in\omega^{\leqslant n}}
from
\mathcal{X}
where

(1)

for
\eta\in\omega^{<n}
, the sets
(\tilde{X}_{\eta,i})_{i<\omega}
are pairwise distinct members of the
\ell
-inconsistent family
(\tilde{X}_{w})_{w\in\tilde{Z}_{\eta}}
, and

(2)

for
\mu\in\omega^{n}
, the set
\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}\tilde{X}_{\eta}
is infinite.

Let
\mu\in\omega^{n}
be given, and let
W\coloneqq\bigcap_{\emptyset\neq\eta\trianglelefteq\mu}\tilde{X}_{\eta}
.
Let
(a,b)\in W^{2}
be independent over the defining parameters for
W
and
\mathcal{Y}
, and let
(z_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a tuple of distinct elements from
Z
.
We set

\tilde{Z}_{\mu}\coloneqq\tilde{Z}_{a,b},\qquad\tilde{X}_{\mu,i}\coloneqq X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}}\text{ for each $i$}.

We’ve already seen that the family
(\tilde{X}_{w})_{w\in\tilde{Z}_{\mu}}=(X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z})_{z\in Z}
is
\ell
-inconsistent.
For
i<\omega
, we have

W\cap\tilde{X}_{\mu,i}=W\cap X^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}}\supseteq W^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}},

and the set
W^{\mathcal{Y}}_{a,b,z_{i}}
is infinite by Lemma
2.2
.
Defining
(\tilde{Z}_{\mu})_{\mu\in\omega^{n}}
and
(\tilde{X}_{\mu,i})_{(\mu,i)\in\omega^{n}\times\omega}
in this way for all
\mu\in\omega^{n}
, we have shown that
\textnormal{\T1\thh}^{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Z}_{X},\ell)\geqslant n+1
, as desired.
∎

Applying Proposition
A.4
, we have:

Corollary 2.11
.

Suppose that
T
is rosy.
Then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
, so
U^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}({\bar{a}}|A)=\rk_{\mathcal{L}}({\bar{a}}|A)
for
{\bar{a}}\in\mathbb{M}^{n}
.

By the main theorem in
[
39
]
(see also
[
26
, Proposition 4.1]
), we also have:

Corollary 2.12
.

Suppose that
T
is rosy.
Then
\mathbb{M}
has bounded Galois group—it has only finitely many extensions of degree
n
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 2.13
.

If
T
is a simple theory in which forking and þ-forking coincide, then one can deduce that
T
is supersimple of SU-rank 1 directly from Theorem
2.10
.
This holds if
T
satisfies the stable forking conjecture
[
38
]
or eliminates hyperimaginaries
[
15
]
.
We are unsure whether Theorem
2.7
follows from Theorem
2.10
without appealing to these major conjectures for simple theories.

Remark 2.14
.

The analogue of Theorems
2.7
and
2.10
for Kim-independence does not hold, essentially because Kim forking does not satisfy base monotonicity in general.
The analogy would suggest that if a theory of fields
T
is geometric and NSOP
1
, then there is no non-algebraic single-variable formula
\varphi(x)
that Kim-divides.
We will give an example contradicting this.
Let
T_{E}
be the theory of an equivalence relation with infinitely many classes, each of which is infinite, in the language consisting of only a binary relation symbol
E
.
Winkler shows that the disjoint union of ACF and
T_{E}
in the language
\mathcal{L}_{\ring}\cup\{E\}
has a model completion, which we denote by ACF
E

[
47
]
.
This model companion is an
interpolative fusion
in the sense of
[
30
]
, and so it is algebraically bounded and has NSOP
1
by
[
31
, Theorems 1.1(1) and 1.3(2)]
.
Let
M\preccurlyeq\mathbb{M}\models\text{ACF}_{E}
be a small elementary substructure and let
b\in\mathbb{M}\setminus M
belong to a distinct
E
-class from each
a\in M
.
Then the formula
E(x,b)
Kim-divides over
M
in the reduct
T_{E}
, and so it Kim-divides over
M
in ACF
E
by
[
31
, Lemma 2.22]
.

3.
Adding derivations

We assume for the rest of this note that
T
extends the
\mathcal{L}_{\ring}
-theory of fields of characteristic zero.
Let
\mathbb{P}\coloneqq\dcl_{\mathcal{L}}(\emptyset)
, and recall that
T
is
algebraically bounded
if

a\in\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(B)\iff\trdeg(a|\mathbb{P}(B))=0

for all
a\in\mathbb{M}
and
B\subseteq\mathbb{M}
.
We assume that one of the following holds:

(I)

T
is algebraically bounded,
\mathcal{L}
extends
\mathcal{L}_{\ring}
by relation symbols and constant
symbols for elements of
\mathbb{P}
, and
T
has quantifier elimination in the language
\mathcal{L}
.

(II)

T
is o-minimal and
\mathcal{L}
contains a function symbol for each
\mathcal{L}(\emptyset)
-definable function.
Then
T
has quantifier elimination and a universal axiomatization in the language
\mathcal{L}
.

In both cases,
T
is geometric.
We let
T^{\forall}
be the universal theory of
T
.
In case (
I
), models of
T^{\forall}
are relational expansions of
\mathbb{P}
-algebras.
In case (
II
),
T^{\forall}=T
.

Lemma 3.1
.

In case (
I
), the independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
is real-canonical.

Proof.

Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
and
C_{1},C_{2}\subseteq B\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be given, and suppose
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C_{i}}B
for
i=1,2
.
We may assume that
C_{i}=\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(C_{i})
for
i=1,2
and that
B=\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(B)
.
Let
D\coloneqq C_{1}\cap C_{2}
.
We need to show that
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{D}B
.
Let
{\bar{a}}
be a tuple from
A
and assume that
{\bar{a}}
is
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}
-dependent over
B
.
Then
{\bar{a}}
is field-theoretically algebraically dependent over
B
, since
T
is algebraically bounded.
Let
V
be the Zariski locus of
{\bar{a}}
over
B
(the smallest Zariski closed set defined over
B
containing
{\bar{a}}
).
Since
B
is a relatively algebraically closed subfield of
\mathbb{M}
,
V
is absolutely irreducible over
B
.
Let
B_{0}\subseteq B
be the smallest field of definition for
V
.
Since
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C_{i}}B
, we have
B_{0}\subseteq C_{i}
for
i=1,2
, so
B_{0}\subseteq D
.
Thus
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}({\bar{a}}|D)=\dim V=\rk_{\mathcal{L}}({\bar{a}}|B)
.
∎

Note that the above lemma may fail in case (
II
).
Indeed, Pillay shows that for
T=\Th(\mathbb{R},+,\cdot,\exp|_{[0,1]})
, the relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
is not real-canonical
[
41
]
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Suppose
T
is algebraically bounded and rosy.
Then
T
has GEI.

Proof.

By Corollary
2.11
and Lemma
3.1
,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\textnormal{\T1\thh}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
is strict and real-canonical.
The corollary then follows from
[
48
, Lemma 3.1]
.
∎

Definition 3.3
.

A
T
-derivation on
K
is a map
\delta\colon K\to K
such that:

(1)

In case (
I
),
\delta
is a derivation on
K
.

(2)

In case (
II
),
\delta
satisfies the chain rule:

\delta f({\bar{u}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}({\bar{u}})\delta u_{i}

for all
{\bar{u}}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in K^{n}
and all function symbols
f\in\mathcal{L}
that are differentiable on an open neighborhood of
{\bar{u}}
.

Let
\Delta=(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{m})
and let
\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}=\mathcal{L}\cup\Delta
.
We let
T^{\Delta,\operatorname{nc}}
be the

\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}
-theory extending
T
by axioms stating that
\Delta
is a tuple of
T
-derivations, and we let
T^{\Delta}
further extend
T^{\Delta,\operatorname{nc}}
by axioms stating that
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{m}
all commute with each other.
We let
T^{\Delta,?}
be one of
T^{\Delta},T^{\Delta,\operatorname{nc}}
.
In case (
I
), we “hardcode” the restriction of
\Delta
to
\mathbb{P}
into the theory
T^{\Delta,?}
, as is done in
[
18
]
.
Given
K\models T^{\forall}
, an element
a\in K
, and a tuple of
T
-derivations
\Delta
on
K
, we set
\Delta(a)\coloneqq(\delta_{1}a,\ldots,\delta_{m}a)\in K^{m}
.
We say that
\Delta
commutes at
a
if
\delta_{i}\delta_{j}a=\delta_{j}\delta_{i}a
for all
i,j
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
K\subseteq_{\mathcal{L}}M
be models of
T^{\forall}
, let
\Delta_{0}
be a tuple of
T
-derivations on
K
, and let
A\subseteq M
be an
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}
-basis for
M
over
K
.
Then for any map
s\colon A\to M^{m}
, there is a unique tuple of
T
-derivations
\Delta
on
M
that extends
\Delta_{0}
and satisfies
s(a)=\Delta(a)
for
a\in A
.
Moreover, if
\Delta_{0}
commutes and
\Delta
commutes at each
a\in A
, then
\Delta
commutes.

Proof.

In case (
I
), this is basic differential algebra; see
[
18
, Fact 3.8]
.
In case (
II
), this follows by
[
16
, Lemmas 2.13 and 6.1]
.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
M\models T
and consider the following diagram of substructures:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K_{1};\Delta_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K_{0};\Delta_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(K_{1},K_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K_{2};\Delta_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}}

Above, unlabeled arrows denote
\mathcal{L}
-substructures.
Suppose that
K_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{K_{0}}K_{2}
.
Then there is a tuple of
T
-derivations
\Delta
on
M
extending
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
and making
M
a model of
T^{\Delta,\operatorname{nc}}
.
Moreover

(1)

If
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
are both commuting tuples of
T
-derivations, then
\Delta
can also be chosen to commute.

(2)

L
is closed under the
T
-derivations in
\Delta
, and the restriction of
\Delta
to
L
is the unique common extension of
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
to a tuple of
T
-derivations on
L
.

Proof.

Let
A_{i}\subseteq K_{i}
be an
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}
-basis for
K_{i}
over
K_{0}
for
i=1,2
.
Then
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
is an
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}
-basis for
L
over
K_{0}
, since
K_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{K_{0}}K_{2}
.
Let
B
be an
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}
-basis for
M
over
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(K_{1},K_{2})
, so

A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup B
is an
\acl_{\mathcal{L}}
-basis for
M
over
K_{0}
.
We extend
\Delta_{0}
to a tuple
of
T
-derivations
\Delta
on
M
by taking
\Delta(a)=\Delta_{i}(a)
for
a\in A_{i}
and
\Delta(b)=0
for
b\in B
.
The corollary follows from the previous lemma.
∎

Note that the above corollary gives precisely the conditions for being “derivation-like” in
[
33
]
.

Corollary 3.6
.

The theories
T^{\Delta,\operatorname{nc}}
and
T^{\Delta}
are derivation-like with respect to
(T,\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\ )
.

We now let
T^{\Delta,?}_{g}
denote the model completion of
T^{\Delta,?}
.
The existence of
T^{\Delta,?}_{g}
in case (
I
) is established in
[
18
]
and existence of
T^{\Delta}_{g}
in case (
II
) is established in
[
16
]
.
The existence of
T^{\Delta,\operatorname{nc}}_{g}
in case (
II
) is established in Appendix
B
below.
Let
(\mathbb{M};\Delta)
be a monster model of
T^{\Delta,?}_{g}
, so
\mathbb{M}
is a monster model of
T
.
We write
\acl_{\Delta}
in place of
\acl_{\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}}
, likewise with
\dcl_{\Delta}
.
For
A\subseteq\mathbb{M}
, we set

\Jet(A)\coloneqq\{\delta_{i_{1}}\delta_{i_{2}}\cdots\delta_{i_{n}}a:\text{$a\in A$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$, and $i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}$}\},

so
\Jet(A)
is the closure of
A
under the derivations in
\Delta
.
We write
\Jet(a)
in place of
\Jet(\{a\})
.
We define the relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\,
on
(\mathbb{M};\Delta)
by

\textstyle A\ \mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}\ B\iff\Jet(A)\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{\Jet(C)}\Jet(B).

In case (
I
), Fornasiero and Terzo show that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\,
is an independence relation
[
20
]
.
Their methods work just as well in case (
II
), using Corollary
3.5
to establish extension.
An alternative proof of this fact can be obtained using Corollary
3.6
and the results of León Sánchez and Mohamed
[
33
]
.

Corollary 3.7

(
[
20
, Theorem 6.1]
,
[
33
, Theorem 3.12]
)
.

\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\,
is a real-strict independence relation.
If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\
is real-canonical with respect to
T
(in particular, if we are in case (
I
)), then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\,
is real-canonical with respect to
T^{\Delta,?}_{g}
.

The independence relation given in
[
33
]
is defined using
\acl_{\Delta}
, and does not
a priori
agree with the relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}\,
above.
To establish this coincidence, one needs the following:

Corollary 3.8

(
[
20
, Corollary 6.2]
,
[
33
, Lemma 3.8]
, Corollary
B.9
)
.

Let
A\subseteq(\mathbb{M};\Delta)\models T^{\Delta,?}_{g}
.
Then
\acl_{\Delta}(A)=\acl_{\mathcal{L}}(\Jet(A))
.
Consequently, we have

\textstyle A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B\iff\acl_{\Delta}(AC)\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\operatorname{M}}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{\acl_{\Delta}(C)}\acl_{\Delta}(BC)

for small sets
A,B,C\subseteq\mathbb{M}
.

We show below that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{${}^{\Delta}$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}
is actually the coarsest real-strict independence relation on
(\mathbb{M};\Delta)
.
For this, we need a couple preliminary results.
Let
\ker(\Delta)\coloneqq\bigcap_{\delta\in\Delta}\ker(\delta)
be the
field of absolute constants
.
Given
a\in\mathbb{M}
, we let
p_{a}^{-}(x)
and
p_{a}^{+}(x)
be the complete unary
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{M})
-types determined by the sets of formulas
\{b<x<a:b\in\mathbb{M}^{<a}\}
and
\{a<x<b:b\in\mathbb{M}^{>a}\}
, respectively.

Lemma 3.9
.

Suppose we are in case (
II
), so
T
is o-minimal.
Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}
be an independence relation on
(\mathbb{M};\Delta)
, let
A,B,C\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be small sets with
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
, and let
a\in A
.
Then there is
d\in\ker(\Delta)
realizing
p_{a}^{+}|_{ABC}
with
Ad\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
, and likewise for
p_{a}^{-}|_{ABC}
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma for
p_{a}^{+}
, with the same proof working for
p_{a}^{-}
.
Full existence gives an
\mathcal{L}^{\Delta}(AC)
-automorphism
\sigma
with
\sigma(B)\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{AC}B
.
Then
\sigma(B)\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}A
as well and applying normality, transitivity, and symmetry, we get

\textstyle ABC\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}\sigma(B),\qquad A\sigma(B)C\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B.

The axioms of
T^{\Delta,?}_{g}
tell us that there is a realization of
p_{a}^{+}|_{ABC}
in
\ker(\Delta)
, likewise for
p_{a}^{+}|_{A\sigma(B)C}
.
Full existence allows us to find realizations
d_{1}\models p_{a}^{+}|_{ABC}
and
d_{2}\models p_{a}^{+}|_{A\sigma(B)C}
with

\textstyle d_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{ABC}\sigma(B),\qquad d_{2}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{A\sigma(B)C}B.

Applying normality, transitivity, and monotonicity to these relations and the ones above, we get

\textstyle Ad_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}\sigma(B),\qquad Ad_{2}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B.

If
d_{2}\leqslant d_{1}
, then
d_{2}\models p_{a}^{+}|_{ABC}
as well, and we may take
d\coloneqq d_{2}
.
If
d_{1}<d_{2}
, then applying
\sigma^{-1}
gives

\textstyle\sigma^{-1}(d_{1})\models\sigma^{-1}(p_{a}^{+}|_{A\sigma(B)C})=p_{a}^{+}|_{ABC},\qquad A\sigma^{-1}(d_{1})\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B,

so we may take
d\coloneqq\sigma^{-1}(d_{1})
.
∎

Corollary 3.10
.

Suppose we are in case (
II
), let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}
be an independence relation on
(\mathbb{M};\Delta)
, let
A,B,C\subseteq\mathbb{M}
be small sets with
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\kern 4.53473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 2.71246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 4.53473pt}{\kern 3.95836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hbox to0.0pt{$$}\hss}\lower 1.93747pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 3.95836pt}}_{C}B
, and let
{\bar{a}}\in A^{n}
with
\rk_{\mathcal{L}}({\bar{a}}|BC)=n
.
Then there is a finite
D\subseteq\ker(\Delta)
and an
\mathcal{L}(D)
-definable open
U\subseteq\mathbb{M}^{n}
such that

(1)

\rk_{\mathcal{L}}(D|ABC)=|D|
and
